\varrho^{\prime}_{n}(x)=\frac{({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x){\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)+{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)}{({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)+{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x))^{2}},

which is less than
|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)|
in absolute value. However, Lemma
C.6
implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]}\Big(|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)|\Big)=O_{p}(n^{p/5}).

(69)

Therefore, on
[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]
, the derivative of
\varrho_{n}
is uniformly bounded by an
O_{p}(n^{p/5})
term. The same bound can be proved for
1-\varrho_{n}
as well.
Noting
\varrho_{n}
is a fraction, we also deduce that
\|\varrho_{n}\|_{\infty}
and
\|1-\varrho_{n}\|_{\infty}
are bounded by one.
For a convex set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}
and a number
M>0
, define the class of functions
\mathcal{D}_{n,M}(\mathcal{X})
by

\mathcal{D}_{n,M}(\mathcal{X})=\bigg\{h:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\ \bigg|\ h\text{ is differentiable on }\mathcal{X},\ \sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|h(x)|+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|h^{\prime}(x)|\leq M\bigg\}

As in the proof of Theorem
1
, we let
M_{n}=Cn^{p/5}
where
C>0
is a constant. Our earlier discussion on
\varrho_{n}
indicates that
for sufficiently large
C>0
,
\varrho_{n}
and
1-\varrho_{n}
restricted to
[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]
belongs to
\mathcal{D}_{n,M_{n}}([-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}])

with high probability as
n\to\infty
. Note also that (
69
) implies
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})\in\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})
with high probability for sufficiently large
C>0
, where
\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})
is as defined in (
24
).
Therefore it is not hard to see that for sufficiently large
C>0
,
(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}1_{[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]}\in\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},-\xi_{n},\xi_{n})
with high probability as
n\to\infty
, where for
-\infty\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq\infty
and
C>0
, the class
\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},r_{1},r_{2})
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},r_{1},r_{2})=\bigg\{h:\mathbb{R}\mapsto[-M_{n},M_{n}]\ \bigg|\ h(x)=q_{1}(x)f_{1}(x)+q_{2}(x)f_{2}(x)\text{ for }

\displaystyle\ x\in[-r,r],\text{ and }0\text{ o.w. where }\ q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{n,M_{n}}([r_{1},r_{2}]),\ f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})\bigg\}

(70)

It must be noted that in case of Theorem
1
, we had
\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})
. Thus in Theorem
2
,
\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n})
is replaced by
\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},-\xi_{n},\xi_{n})
.

Corollary 2.7.2 of
Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
implies

\sup_{Q}\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{D}_{n,M_{n}}([r_{1},r_{2}]),L_{2}(Q))\lesssim\frac{(r_{2}-r_{1})M_{n}}{\epsilon},

where the supremum is over all probability measure
Q
on real line.
On the other hand, (
26
) implies
\sup_{Q}\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{U}_{n}(M_{n}),L_{2}(Q))\lesssim M_{n}/\epsilon
. Furthermore, (
27
) entails that the bracketing entropy of the function-class
\mathcal{F}_{I}
, consisting of indicator functions of the form
1_{[r_{1},r_{2}]}
, is of the order
\epsilon^{-1}
. Therefore we can show that

\sup_{Q}\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},r_{1},r_{2}),L_{2}(Q))\lesssim\frac{(r_{2}-r_{1})M_{n}}{\epsilon}.

(71)

Next, we replace the class
\mathcal{H}_{n}(C)
in the proof of Theorem
1
by the class

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{sym}_{n}(C)=\bigg\{h:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\ \bigg|

\displaystyle\ h(x)=(u(x)-{\phi}_{0}^{\prime}(x))1_{[r_{1},r_{2}]}(x),\ u\in\mathcal{U}^{sym}_{n}(M_{n},r_{1},r_{2}),

\displaystyle\ \|h\|_{P_{0},2}\leq Cn^{-2p/5}(\log n)^{3},\quad\|h\|_{\infty}\leq M_{n},

\displaystyle\ [r_{1},r_{2}]\subset[\theta_{0}-C\log n,\theta_{0}+C\log n]\cap\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(\phi_{0}))\bigg\},

where we substituted the class
\mathcal{U}_{n}(C)
in
\mathcal{H}_{n}(C)
by the class
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{sym}(C,r_{1},r_{2})
. Although the dependence of
M_{n}
on
C
is suppressed by its notation, the former is a function of
C
and
n
. This validates that the set
\mathcal{H}^{sym}_{n}(C)
depends only on
C
and
n
, as indicated by the notation.
Note that
[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(\psi_{0}))
by Lemma
A.2
, and
\xi_{n}
is
O_{p}(\log n)
by Lemma
A.4
.
Therefore proceeding as in Theorem
1
, but replacing Lemma
A.16
by Lemma
C.7
, we can also show that the function

h_{n}(x)=((\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x-\bar{\theta}_{n})-\phi_{0}^{\prime}(x))1_{[\bar{\theta}_{n}-\xi_{n},\bar{\theta}_{n}+\xi_{n}]}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

(72)

is a member of
\mathcal{H}^{sym}_{n}(C)
with high probability for sufficiently large
n
.
Using (
71
) in conjuction with (
27
) we can show that

\sup_{Q}\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{H}^{sym}_{n}(C),L_{2}(Q))\lesssim\frac{C(\log n)M_{n}}{\epsilon}.

(73)

Since the bracketing entropy of
\mathcal{H}^{sym}_{n}(C)
differs from that of
\mathcal{H}_{n}(C)
only by a poly-log term, so does the entropy integral. Also, noting
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
yields a consistent
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\eta_{n})
(see Lemma
C.5
) analogous to the log-concave
\tilde{g}_{n}
’s, rest of the proof of
T_{1}=o_{p}(1)
follows in a similar fashion as that of Theorem
1
.

Step two: showing
T_{2n}\to_{p}-1
:

Recall the function
b_{n}
defined in (
29
). Because
T_{2n}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}b_{n}(t)dt
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{Y}_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}b_{n}(t)dt\to_{a.s.}1
. The proof is not much different from the proof of Lemma
A.1
. We will only point out where the current proof differs from the proof of Lemma
A.1
.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}
are as defined in the proof of Lemma
A.1
.
Let us also introduce the integrals

\mathcal{I}^{+}_{1n}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}\tilde{\phi}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)^{2}\widehat{h}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)dt,\quad\mathcal{I}^{+}_{2n}=\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{A}_{n}+\tilde{\delta}_{n}}\tilde{\phi}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)^{2}\widehat{h}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)dt,

\mathcal{I}^{-}_{1n}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}\tilde{\phi}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)^{2}\widehat{h}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)dt,\quad\ \mathcal{I}^{-}_{2n}=\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{A}_{n}+\tilde{\delta}_{n}}\tilde{\phi}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)^{2}\widehat{h}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)dt.

The above integrals replace the integrals
\mathcal{I}_{1n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2n}
in the proof of Lemma
A.1
.
We also define

\mathcal{J}^{+}_{n}=\frac{(\log n)^{2}H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})^{2}}{\inf_{x\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}}{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)},\quad\mathcal{J}^{-}_{n}=\frac{(\log n)^{2}H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})^{2}}{\inf_{x\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\prime}}{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)}

Similar to Lemma
A.1
, it can be shown that it suffices to show that every subsequence has a further subsequence
n_{k}
, along which,
\mathbb{Y}_{n_{k}}\to_{a.s.}1
. We claim that given any sequence, there exists a subsequence
n_{k}
such that the set
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
has probability one, where we define
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
to be the set on which the following hold:

(a)
\overline{\theta}_{n_{k}}\to_{k}\theta_{0}
, (b)
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{n_{k}}(\eta_{n_{k}})\to_{k}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}
, (c)
\xi_{n_{k}}\to_{k}G_{0}^{-1}(1)
, (d)
\omega_{n_{k}}\to\omega_{0}
, (e)
\mathcal{J}_{n_{k}}^{+}
,
\mathcal{J}_{n_{k}}^{-}\to_{k}0
,
(f)
\mathcal{I}^{+}_{in_{k}}
,
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{in_{k}}\to_{k}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}
for
i=1,2
, (g)
\|\widehat{h}_{n_{k}}^{sm}(\overline{\theta}_{n_{k}}\pm\mathord{\cdot})-g_{0}\|_{\infty}\to 0
, (h)
\|\tilde{g}_{n_{k}}^{sym,sm}-g_{0}\|_{\infty}\to 0
, (i)
\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(\psi_{0}))
for all sufficiently large
k
.

Note that
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
is similar to the good set
\mathcal{M}
in the proof of Lemma
A.1
.
The claim that there exists a sequence
n_{k}
so that
P(\mathcal{M}^{sym})=1
can be verified using Fact
6
in the same way we verified a similar claim for
\mathcal{M}
. The only difference is that here we require Lemma
C.5
for (b), Lemma
C.6
instead of Lemma
A.7
for (e), and Lemma
C.5
instead of Lemma
A.14
for (f). As in Lemma
A.1
, we will show that
\mathbb{Y}_{n_{k}}\to_{k}1
on
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
. For the sake of simplicity, we drop
k
from the subscripts.

The pointwise converges of
b_{n}
can be proved along the lines of (
34
). However, Lemma
A.8
can not be directly applied this time because
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
is not log-concave. On the other hand, Lemma
A.8
does apply to
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
, because the latter is log-concave.
Exploiting the connection between
(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}
and
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}
as given by (
56
), and arguing as in the proof of Proposition
2
, we can show that the assertions of Lemma
A.8
still hold for
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
on
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
. Thus (
34
) holds for
b_{n}
in case of
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
.

However, we can not bound
b_{n}
using (
35
) because
(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}
is not monotone. However,
using (
56
), we can still bound

\displaystyle\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+\tilde{\delta}_{n}}(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(z)dz\bigg|\leq

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+\tilde{\delta}_{n}}\Big(|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+z)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-z)|\Big)dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\delta}_{n}\Big(\max\{|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t+\tilde{\delta}_{n})|,|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)|\}

\displaystyle\ +\max\{|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t-\tilde{\delta}_{n})|,|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)|\}\Big).

Using the above, it can be shown that
|b_{n}(t)|\leq|b_{n}^{+}(t)|+|b_{n}^{-}(t)|
, where

\displaystyle b_{n}^{+}(t)=

\displaystyle\ 1_{A_{n}}(t)|\psi_{0}^{\prime}(t)|g_{0}(t)\Big(|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+t+\tilde{\delta}_{n})|\Big)/\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\eta_{n}),

\displaystyle b_{n}^{-}(t)=

\displaystyle\ 1_{A_{n}}(t)|\psi_{0}^{\prime}(t)|g_{0}(t)\Big(|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-t-\tilde{\delta}_{n})|\Big)/\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\eta_{n}).

The proof will be complete by Pratt’s Lemma (Fact
10
) if we can show that there exists integrable functions
c_{n}^{+}
,
c_{n}^{-}
,
c^{+}
and
c^{-}
so that
|b_{n}^{+}|\leq c_{n}^{+}
,
|b_{n}^{-}|\leq c_{n}^{-}
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}c_{n}^{+}(t)dt\to_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}c^{+}(t)dt
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}c_{n}^{-}(t)dt\to_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}c^{-}(t)dt
, and
c_{n}^{+}\to_{n}c^{+}
and
c_{n}^{-}\to_{n}c^{-}
almost everywhere Lebesgue on
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
. The functions
c_{n}^{+}
and
c_{n}^{-}
can be constructed in the same way we constructed
c_{n}
for bounding
b_{n}
in the proof of Lemma
A.1
. Since the proof follows in a similar manner by replacing
\mathcal{M}
by
\mathcal{M}^{sym}
, and
\tilde{g}_{n}
by
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
, it is skipped.
\Box

C.1
Auxiliary lemma for Theorem
2

In this subsection,
\xi_{n}
will generally refer to
\xi_{n}(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})\equiv(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
. Although
\tilde{g}_{n}
can be either
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
or
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
, its definition should be clear from the context.

C.1.1
Lemmas on Hellinger error of
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
:

Lemma C.1
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
2
,
H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
and
H(\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm},g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

First of all note that
2H^{2}({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}},f_{0})^{2}
is bounded by
\|{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}-f_{0}\|_{1}
which is not larger than

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{\edint\slb\hf(x-t)-f_0(x-t)\srb\varphi(t/\smbn)dt}dx

\displaystyle\ +\widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{ \edint\slb f_0(x)-f_0(x-t)\srb\varphi(t/\smbn)dt}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\widehat{h}_{n}-f_{0}\|_{1}+\widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{\edint\varphi(t/\smbn)\dint_{x-t}^x f_0'(z)dzdt }dx,

whose first term can be bounded using Fact
5
, which yields

\|\widehat{h}_{n}-f_{0}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{2}H(\widehat{h}_{n},f_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-2/5})

by Theorem 3.2 of
Doss and Wellner 2016
. The second term

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{\edint\varphi(t/\smbn)\dint_{x-t}^x f_0'(z)dzdt }dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\displaystyle\int_{x}^{x-t}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x-t}^{x}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdt\bigg)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x}^{x+t}\varphi(-t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x-t}^{x}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdt\bigg)dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x-|t|}^{x+|t|}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdtdx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x-|t|}^{x+|t|}\absolutevalue{f_0'(z)}dzdxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|t|\widehat{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}\varphi(t/\widehat{\lambda}_{n})\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f_{0}^{\prime}(z)|dzdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 4\widehat{\lambda}_{n}E[|\mathbb{Z}|]f_{0}(\theta_{0})

where
\mathbb{Z}\sim N(0,1)
. In the last step we used the fact
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{0}^{\prime}(z)|dz=2f_{0}(\theta_{0})
which follows because
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
Thus

H^{2}({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}},f_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-2/5})+O_{p}(\widehat{\lambda}_{n}).

(74)

Our next step is finding the rate of
\widehat{\lambda}_{n}
. To that end, note that because
\int_{R}x\widehat{h}_{n}(x)dx
is the sample average
(
Dümbgen and Rufibach 2009
, Corollary 2.3 of)
, (
10
) implies that

\widehat{\lambda}^{2}_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{2}d(\mathbb{F}_{n}-\widehat{H}_{n})
where
\widehat{H}_{n}
is the distribution function of
\widehat{h}_{n}
.
Therefore,

\widehat{b}^{2}_{n}\leq\absolutevalue{\edint z^2d(\mathbb{F}_n-F_0)}+\absolutevalue{\edint z^2d(\hnn-F_0)},

whose first term is
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by the central limit theorem because
F_{0}
has finite second central moment. On the other hand, since the second term equals

\absolutevalue{\edint z^2(\sqrt{\hf(z)}-\sqrt{f_0(z)})(\sqrt{\hf(z)}+\sqrt{f_0(z)})dz},

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality indicates that
its square is bounded by

4H(\widehat{h}_{n},f_{0})^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}z^{4}(\widehat{h}_{n}(z)+f_{0}(z))dz.

The fourth moment of
f_{0}
is finite by Fact
1
. On the other hand, Theorem 4 of
Cule and Samworth 2010
implies that there exists
a>0
so that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{a|z|}|\widehat{h}_{n}(z)-f_{0}(z)|dz\to_{a.s.}0.

Therefore it follows that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{4}\widehat{h}_{n}(z)dz=O_{p}(1)
. Thus, we conclude
\widehat{\lambda}_{n}
is

O_{p}(1)H(\widehat{h}_{n},f_{0})
, which is
O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
.
Therefore, (
74
) yields that
H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}},f_{0})
is
O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
. Since the Hellinger distance is translation invariant,

H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}},g_{0}(-\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}))\leq H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}},f_{0})+H(g_{0}(-\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}),f_{0}),

whose first term is
O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
, and second term is
O_{p}(|\bar{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}|)
by Fact
15
. Because
\bar{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
,
H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
follows. Since
g_{0}
is symmetric about zero, we can show that
H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-1/5})
as well. Since
2g_{0}(x)=g_{0}(x)+g_{0}(-x)
, and

(\sqrt{a+b}-\sqrt{c+d})^{2}\lesssim(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{c})^{2}+(\sqrt{b}-\sqrt{d})^{2}\quad\text{ for }a,b,c,d>0,

it follows that

H({\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}},g_{0})\lesssim H(\widehat{h}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})+H(\widehat{h}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-1/5}).

∎

C.1.2
Lemmas on distance between
\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}
and
\widehat{H}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
:

Lemma C.2
.

Under the set up of Theorem
2
,

(A)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \|\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}-\widehat{H}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(n^{-p})

(B)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \sup_{x\in[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]}{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)^{-1}=O_{p}(\eta_{n}^{-1}),

where
p=1/5
and
\xi_{n}=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
.

Proof of Lemma
C.2
.

From the definition of total variation distance, it follows that

\|\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}-\widehat{H}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})\|_{\infty}\leq d_{TV}(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n},\widehat{H}^{sm}_{n}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}))
, which equals

\displaystyle 2\|{\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}}-{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{2}H({\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}},{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})),

where the last step follows by Fact
5
. The proof of part (A) then follows noting

H({\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}},{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}))\leq H({\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}},g_{0})+H({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}),g_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-p})

by Lemma
C.1
.

For the proof of part (B), note that since

{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}
is log-concave, it attains its minimum on any interval at one of the endpoints.
Therefore

\inf_{x\in[-\xi_{n},\xi_{n}]}{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)=\min({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\xi_{n}),{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\xi_{n})).

Using Fact
4
in step (a), and part A of the current lemma in step (b), we can show that

{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\xi_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\omega_{n}\widehat{H}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\xi_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\omega_{n}(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}(-\xi_{n})-O_{p}(n^{-p})),

which, by definition of
\xi_{n}
, equals
\omega_{n}\eta_{n}-O_{p}(n^{-p})
. Since
\omega_{n}\to_{p}\omega_{0}>0
by Fact
4
, and
\eta_{n}=Cn^{-2p/5}
, it follows that
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\xi_{n})^{-1}=O_{p}(\eta_{n}^{-1})
. In a similar way, it can be shown that
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\xi_{n})^{-1}=O_{p}(\eta_{n}^{-1})
. Therefore, the proof follows.
∎

C.1.3
Lemmas on
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}
and
(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}
:

Lemma C.3
.

The conditions of
Lemma
A.12
and Lemma
A.13
hold for

a_{n}=\xi_{n}\equiv\xi_{n}(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
and
\tilde{g}_{n}={\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
.

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

Note that since
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
satisfies Condition
3
, Lemma
A.9
entails that
a_{n}=\xi_{n}
satisfies (
45
). Moreover, Lemma
C.2
(B) indicates that the supremum of
-\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
over
[-a_{n},a_{n}]
is
O_{p}(\log n)
for the above choice of
a_{n}
. Also because
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}
satisfies Condition
1
,
\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm}
is bounded above. Therefore we obtain that the supremum of
|\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})|
on
[-a_{n},a_{n}]
is
O_{p}(\log n)
. Thus, we conclude that (
43
) holds for our choice of
a_{n}
and
\tilde{g}_{n}
. As a result, this
(a_{n},\tilde{g}_{n})
pair satisfies the conditions of Lemma
A.12
.

For Lemma
A.13
, first note that
a_{n}=O_{p}(\log n)
by Lemma
A.4
. Noting
\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})=\text{supp}({\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}})=\mathbb{R}
, we also obtain that (
46
) holds with probability tending to one because

P\Big([-\xi_{n}-\eta_{n}/\log n,\xi_{n}+\eta_{n}/\log_{n}]\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(\psi_{0}))\Big)\to 1

by Lemma
A.6
.
Thus the conditions of Lemma
A.13
are satisfied if
(
47
) holds for
(\xi_{n},{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}))
.
Now
by Lemma
C.2
, with probability tending to one,

\widehat{H}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-a_{n})\geq\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}(-a_{n})-o_{p}(n^{-p})=\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}))-o_{p}(n^{-p}),

which is
\eta_{n}-o_{p}(n^{-p})
. Since
\eta_{n}
is
O(n^{-2p/5})
, it follows that

P\Big(\widehat{H}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-a_{n})\geq\eta_{n}/4\Big)\to 1.

Similarly we can show that

P\Big(1-\widehat{H}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+a_{n})\geq\eta_{n}/4\Big)\to 1,

which implies (
47
)
holds for
a_{n}=\xi_{n}
when
\tilde{g}_{n}={\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
. The proof for
\tilde{g}_{n}={\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\mathord{\cdot})
follows in a similar way, which completes the proof of the current lemma.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Suppose
\mu_{n}
is a density so that
\|\mu_{n}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(1)
.
Let

\xi_{n}=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
. Then
p=1/5
satisfies

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}((\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{2}\mu_{n}(x)dx=O_{p}((\log n)^{6}n^{-4p/5}).

Proof of lemma
C.4
.

Using the representation of
(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}
given by (
56
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}\Big((\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(x)\Big)^{2}\mu_{n}(x)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}\Big((\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)-\varrho_{n}(x)\psi^{\prime}_{0}(x)+(1-\varrho_{n}(x))\psi_{0}^{\prime}(-x)\Big)^{2}\mu_{n}(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 2\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}\varrho_{n}(x)^{2}\Big((\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(x)\Big)^{2}\mu_{n}(x)dx

\displaystyle\ +2\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}(1-\varrho_{n}(x))^{2}\Big((\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(-x)\Big)^{2}\mu_{n}(x)dx

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\ 2\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}\bigg\{\Big((\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(x)\Big)^{2}+\Big((\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)-\psi^{\prime}_{0}(-x)\Big)^{2}\bigg\}\mu_{n}(x)dx

which is
O_{p}((\log n)^{6}n^{-4p/5})
by Lemma
C.3
and Lemma
A.13
. Here
(a) follows because
\varrho_{n}
is a fraction.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Suppose
\eta_{n}=Cn^{-2p/5}
, where
p=1/5
and
C>0
.
Let

\xi_{n}=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
. Then under the set up of Theorem
2
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)^{2}{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)\to_{p}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}},

(75)

and

\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)\to_{p}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}.

(76)

Proof of Lemma
C.5
.

It suffices to show that the pairs
(\xi_{n},{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot}))
and
(\xi_{n},\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm})
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
A.14
.
By Lemma
C.3
,
a_{n}=\xi_{n}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
A.13
, which entails that (a)
a_{n}
is
O_{p}(\log n)
and (b) (
46
) holds for
a_{n}
with probability tending to one, where (
46
) implies
[-a_{n},a_{n}]\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(\psi_{0}))
.
Next, the condition
a_{n}=\xi_{n}\to_{p}G_{0}^{-1}(1)
holds by Lemma
A.3
. Finally, (
53
) holds for
{\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm\mathord{\cdot})
and
\tilde{g}_{n}^{sym,sm}
by Lemma
C.3
and Lemma
C.4
, respectively.
Therefore the proof follows from
Lemma
A.14
.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

Let
\xi_{n}=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n})
where
\eta_{n}=Cn^{-2p/5}
for some
C>0
and
p=1/5
.
Suppose
y_{n}
is a sequence of random variables such that

P(|y_{n}|\leq\eta_{n}/(2g_{0}(0)))\to 1
.
Then under the conditions of Theorem
2
, we have

\sup_{x\in[-\xi_{n}-y_{n},\xi_{n}+y_{n}]}\bigg\{|(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)|+|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm x)|\bigg\}=O_{p}(n^{p/5}).

Proof of Lemma
C.6
.

Note that (
56
) implies

|(\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)|\leq\max\bigg\{|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+x)|,|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-x)|\bigg\}.

Therefore it suffices to bound
|(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}\pm)|
only. Since the proof of
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
and
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}-\mathord{\cdot})
are similar, we only show the proof for
(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
.
Denoting
\tilde{g}_{n}={\widehat{h}_{n}^{sm}}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
,
\tilde{\psi}_{n}=(\tilde{\phi}_{n}^{sm})^{\prime}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
, and
\tilde{G}_{n}=\widehat{H}_{n}^{sm}(\bar{\theta}_{n}+\mathord{\cdot})
, note that
the following holds for any
q\in(0,1/2)
by Fact
8
because
\tilde{g}_{n}
is positive on
J(\tilde{G}_{n})
:

\displaystyle\int_{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q/2)}^{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q)}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}(x)dx=\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{G}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q/2))}^{\tilde{G}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q))}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n}^{-1}(z))^{2}dz.

Because
(\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}
is non-increasing on
(-\infty,0]
, the above yields

\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q))^{2}\leq\frac{\displaystyle\int_{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q/2)}^{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q)}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}(x)dx}{\tilde{G}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q))-\tilde{G}_{n}((\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(q/2))}.

Letting
q=\eta_{n}/2
, and denoting
\tilde{\xi}_{n}=(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n}/2)
, we obtain that

\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(\tilde{\xi}_{n})^{2}\leq\displaystyle\int_{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}/4)}^{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}/2)}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}(x)dx\Big/\Big(\eta_{n}/4-2\|\tilde{G}_{n}-\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}\|_{\infty}\Big).

Now Lemma
C.2
implies

\eta_{n}/4-2\|\tilde{G}_{n}-\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n}\|_{\infty}=\eta_{n}/4-O_{p}(n^{-p}),

whose dominating term is
\eta_{n}/4
. Also

\displaystyle\int_{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}/4)}^{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}/2)}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}(x)dx\leq\displaystyle\int_{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(\eta_{n}/4)}^{(\tilde{G}^{sym,sm}_{n})^{-1}(1-\eta_{n}/4)}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}(x)^{2}\tilde{g}_{n}(x)dx

which converges in probability to
\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}
by Lemma
C.5
.
Therefore
\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{n}({\tilde{\xi}_{n}})
is
O_{p}(\eta_{n}^{-1/2})
. The rest of the proof follows similar to the proof of Lemma
A.15
.
∎

\displaystyle\int_{-\xi_{n}}^{\xi_{n}}((\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{sym,sm})^{\prime}(x)-\psi_{0}^{\prime}(z-\tilde{\delta}_{n}))^{2}f_{0}(z+\bar{\theta}_{n})dz=O_{p}(n^{-4p/5}(\log n)^{6}).

instead of Lemma
A.13
to bound
T_{1}

D
Proof of Lemma
1

Proof of Lemma
1
.

For
k\geq 1
, we denote
A_{k}
to be the set
[-1/(2k),1/(2k)]
, and consider
the sequence of functions
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
defined by

\psi_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}\begin{matrix}\log k,&\quad x\in A_{k}\\
-\infty,&\quad\text{o.w.}\end{matrix}\end{cases}

Observe that

\Psi_{n}(x_{0},\psi_{k})=\log k-1\to\infty,\quad\text{ as }k\to\infty.

Therefore,
x_{0}
indeed is a candidate for the MLE of
\theta_{0}
. However, the MLE of
\psi_{0}
, i.e.
\widehat{\psi}_{n}
, does not exist in this case. To verify, observe that if
\widehat{\psi}_{n}
does exist for some
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
, we also have

\widehat{\psi}_{n}(x_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\widehat{\psi}_{n}(x)}dx=\Psi_{n}(x_{0},\widehat{\psi}_{n})\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}\Psi_{n}(x_{0},\psi_{k})=\infty,

leading to
\widehat{\psi}_{n}(x_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})=\infty
,
which contradicts the fact that
\widehat{\psi}_{n}
is a proper concave function.
Hence, we conclude that the MLE of
(\theta_{0},\psi_{0})
does not exist when
\mathbb{F}_{n}
is degenerate.
∎

E
Proof of Theorem
3

To prove Theorem
3
, it will be beneficial to prove a general result first. We begin by stating a condition.

Condition 4
.

(Existence of log-concave projection.)

F
is a non-degenerate distribution function with finite first moment.

Any
F
satisfying Condition
4
has a well-defined log-concave projection, i.e. its projection (with respect to the KL divergence) onto the space of all distributions with density in
\mathcal{LC}
is a unique distribution function
(
Dümbgen et al. 2011
, Theorem 2.2,)
.
Note that
\mathbb{F}_{n}
satisfies Condition
4
with probability one.
We will show that for any distribution function
F
satisfying Condition
4
,

(\theta^{*}(F),\psi^{*}(F))=\argmax_{\theta\in\mathbb{R},\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}}\Psi(\theta,\psi,F)

(77)

exists where
\Psi
is the criterion function defined in (
13
).

Proposition 3
.

If
F
satisfies condition
4
, then
\theta^{*}(F)
and
\psi^{*}(F)
exist.

Observe that Proposition
3

implies the first part of Theorem
3
because
if
F=\mathbb{F}_{n}
,
(\theta^{*}(F),\psi^{*}(F))
corresponds to the MLE
(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\widehat{g}_{n})
.
The second part of Theorem
3
follows from Lemma
E.1
, which is proved in Appendix
E.1
.

Lemma E.1
.

Suppose
F
is such that
J(F)=(a,b)
where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
J(F)=\{0<F<1\}
. Then
Under condition
4
, there exists at least one
\theta^{*}(F)
so that
\theta^{*}(F)\in[a,b]
.

Thus it remains to prove Proposition
3
. To that end, we will need a continuity result on the partially maximized criterion function

L(\theta;F)=\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}}\Psi(\theta,\psi,F).

(78)

Lemma E.2
.

Suppose the distribution function
F
satisfies condition
4
. Then the map
\theta\mapsto L(\theta;F)

is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
, where
L(\theta;F)
is as defined in (
78
).

The proof of Lemma
E.2
can be found in Appendix
E.1
.
Now we are ready to prove Proposition
3
.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Let us define

L(F)=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R},\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}}\Psi(\theta,\psi,F).

(79)

Our first step is to show that
L(F)
is finite.
From the definition of
\Psi
in (
13
), it is not hard to see that

L(F)\leq\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{C}}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi(x)dF(x)-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\psi(x)}dx\bigg),

where
\mathcal{C}
denotes the set of all real-valued concave functions. Theorem 2.2 of
Dümbgen et al. 2011
entails that under condition
4
, the term on the right hand side of the above display is finite. Therefore,
L(F)<\infty
follows. To show that
L(F)>-\infty
, we note that the map
x\mapsto-|x|\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
. Therefore, (
13
) and (
79
) lead to

L(F)\geq-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x|dF(x)-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-|x|}dx>-\infty,

which follows from condition
4
. Hence, we conclude that
L(F)\in\mathbb{R}
.

Now we have to show that there exist
\theta^{*}(F)\in\mathbb{R}
and
\psi^{*}(F)\in\mathcal{SLC}_{0}
such that

\Psi(\theta^{*}(F),\psi^{*}(F),F)=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R},\psi\in\mathcal{SLC}_{0}}\Psi(\theta,\psi,F)=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}L(\theta;F)=L(F).

Now there exists a sequence
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
such that
L(\theta_{k};F)\uparrow L(F)
as
k\to\infty
. Suppose
the sequence
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is bounded. Then we can find a subsequence
\{\theta_{k_{r}}\}_{r\geq 1}
converging to some
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the map
L(\theta;F)
is continuous in
\theta
by Lemma
E.2
, we also have

L(\theta^{\prime};F)=\lim_{r\to\infty}L(\theta_{k_{r}};F)=L(F),

which implies that
\theta^{\prime}
is a maximizer of
L(\theta;F)
. Now we invoke Proposition 4(iii) of
Xu and Samworth 2019
, which
states that for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique log-density
{\psi}_{\theta}
, which maximizes
\Psi(\theta,\psi,F)
in
\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
provided
F
satisfies condition
4
. It is not hard to see that
(\theta^{\prime},\psi_{\theta^{\prime}})
will be a candidate for
(\theta^{*}(F),\psi^{*}(F))
. Thus, to complete the proof, it remains to show that
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is bounded. We will show that

\theta_{k}\to_{k}\pm\infty
leads to
L(\theta_{k};F)\to_{k}-\infty
, which contradicts the fact that
L(\theta_{k};F)\to_{k}L(F)\in\mathbb{R}
, thus completing the proof.

Consider
\theta_{k}\to_{k}\pm\infty
. By Proposition 4(iii) of
Xu and Samworth 2019
, for each
\theta_{k}
, there exists a log-density
\psi_{\theta_{k}}\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
such that
L(\theta_{k};F)=\Psi(\theta,\psi_{\theta_{k}},F)
.
Now note that if
e^{\psi}\in\mathcal{SLC}_{0}
, then
\psi
satisfies

2xe^{\psi(x)}\leq\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{x}e^{\psi(z)}dz\leq 1\quad\text{ for any }x\geq 0,

which implies
|\psi(x)|\leq-\log|2x|
. Noting
\psi_{\theta_{k}}\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
for each
k\geq 1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta,\psi_{\theta_{k}},F)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi_{\theta_{k}}(x-\theta)dF(x)-1\leq-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log\bigg(2|x-\theta_{k}|\bigg)dF(x)-1.

Now if
\theta_{k}\to_{k}\pm\infty
, using Fatou’s Lemma, we derive that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}L(\theta_{k};F)\leq-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\bigg(\log|x-\theta_{k}|\bigg)dF(x)-(\log 2+1),

which is
-\infty
.
This leads to the desired contradiction, which completes the proof.
∎

E.1
Auxilliary Lemmas for Theorem
3

Proof of Lemma
E.2
.

Observe that (
13
) implies
\Psi(\theta,\psi,F)
can also be written as

\Psi(\theta,\psi,F)=\Psi(0,\psi,F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta))
.
Hence, to prove Lemma
E.2
, it suffices to show that as
\theta_{k}\to_{k}\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

L(\theta_{k};F)=\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}}\Psi(0,\psi,F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta_{k}))\to_{k}\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}}\Psi(0,\psi,F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta))=L(\theta,F).

Proposition
6
of
Xu and Samworth 2019
implies that under condition
4
, the convergence in the above display holds if the Wasserstein distance

d_{W}(F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta_{k}),F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta))\to_{k}0.

(80)

Now by Theorem
6.9
of
Villani 2009

(see also Theorem
7.12
of
Villani 2003
, )
, (
80
) follows if (a)
F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta_{k})
converges weakly to
F(\mathord{\cdot}+\theta)
as
k\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle(b)\quad\quad\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x|dF(x+\theta_{k})\to_{k}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x|dF(x+\theta).

Now (a) follows noting that for any bounded continuous function
h
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(x-\theta_{k})dF(x)\to_{k}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(x-\theta)dF(x)

by the dominated convergence theorem since
\theta_{k}\to_{k}\theta
.
For proving (b), first notice that
F
has finite first moment by condition
4
. Therefore, another application of the dominated convergence yields that as
\theta_{k}\to_{k}\theta
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x|dF(x+\theta_{k})=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x-\theta_{k}|dF(x)\to_{k}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x|dF(x+\theta),

which proves (b), and thus completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
E.1
.

We will show that if
J(F)=(a,b)
, the functional
\theta\mapsto L(\theta;F)
defined in (
78
) is non-decreasing in
\theta
on
(-\infty,a]
, and non-increasing in
\theta
on
[b,\infty)
. Suppose the above claim holds. Then clearly either
L(\theta,F)
attains its maximum in
[a,b]
or
L(\theta,F)=L(F)
over an interval with nonempty overlap with
[a,b]
. Here
L(F)
is as defined in (
79
).
In either cases, one can find a
\theta^{*}(F)\in[a,b]
, which completes the proof of Lemma
E.1

To show that
L(\theta;F)
is non-decreasing in
\theta
on
(-\infty,a]
, we first note that for
\theta<\theta^{\prime}\leq a
, and
\psi\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\psi(x-\theta)dF(x)\leq\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\psi(x-\theta^{\prime})dF(x),

since
\psi
is non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
, and

0\leq x-\theta^{\prime}<x-\theta

for
x\geq a
.
Therefore, from (
78
), it is not hard to see that
L(\theta;F)\leq L(\theta^{\prime};F)
. Similarly we can show that for
\theta>\theta^{\prime}\geq b
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\psi(x-\theta)dF(x)\leq\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\psi(x-\theta^{\prime})dF(x),

since
\psi
is non-decreasing on
(-\infty,0]
, and
x-\theta<x-\theta^{\prime}\leq 0
for
x\leq b
. Therefore,
L(\theta;F)\leq L(\theta^{\prime};F)
, which completes the proof.
∎

F
Proof of Theorem
5

Before going into the proof, we will introduce some new notations and state some lemmas that will be required later in the proof.
We let
\widehat{G}_{n}
and
\widehat{F}_{n}
denote the distribution functions corresponding to
\widehat{g}_{n}
and
\widehat{f}_{n}
, respectively. Also, we let
\widehat{\phi}_{n}
denote the log-density
\log\widehat{f}_{n}
. Also, we let
\delta_{n}=\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{n}
.

Now we state a lemma which basically says that
\log\widehat{f}_{n}
is uniformly bounded above for sufficiently large
n
with probability one. This lemma is proved in Appendix
F.1
.

Lemma F.1
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
5
,

P\bigg(\sup_{n}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\log\widehat{f}_{n}(x)<\infty\bigg)=1.

We first show that
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\to_{a.s.}\theta_{0}
.
In their proof of Theorem
3.1
,
Pal et al. 2007
show that if a sequence of log-concave functions
\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
(which can be stochastic) satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log f_{n}(X_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log f_{0}(X_{i})

(81)

with probability one, we have
H(f_{n},f_{0})\to_{a.s.}0
, provided

P\bigg(\sup_{x}\log f_{n}(x)=o\bigg(\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\log n}\bigg)\bigg)=1.

If we take
f_{n}=\widehat{f}_{n}
, we have

\sup_{x}\log\widehat{f}_{n}(x)=\widehat{\phi}_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n})=\widehat{\psi}_{n}(0)
.
Lemma
F.1
entails that

P(\limsup_{n}\widehat{\psi}_{n}(0)<\infty)=1
.
Also, note that being the MLE of
f_{0}
,
\widehat{f}_{n}
automatically satisfies (
81
),
which implies

H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})\to_{a.s.}0.

(82)

Denote by
\widehat{F}_{n}
the distribution of
\widehat{f}_{n}
.
Because
d_{TV}(\widehat{F}_{n},F_{0})\leq\sqrt{2}H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})
by Fact
15
, (
82
) implies
d_{TV}(\widehat{F}_{n},F_{0})\to_{a.s.}0
, which indicates
F_{n}\to_{d}F_{0}
almost surely. In that case,
Proposition 2 of
Cule and Samworth 2010
implies that there exists
\alpha>0
so that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\alpha|x|}\absolutevalue{\hgf(x)-f_0(x)}dx\to_{a.s.}0.

Therefore the moments of
\widehat{f}_{n}
converges almost surely to that of
f_{0}
. Notably, the first moment of
f_{0}
is
\theta_{0}
, and because
\widehat{g}_{n}
is symmetric about zero, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\widehat{f}_{n}(x)dx=\widehat{\theta}_{n}+\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})\widehat{g}_{n}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})dx=\widehat{\theta}_{n}.

Thus
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\to_{a.s.}\theta_{0}
follows.
Since
\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}<\infty
, the density
f_{0}
is absolutely continuous
(
Huber 1964
, Theorem 3)
. Because
f_{0}
is continuous, Proposition 2 of
Cule and Samworth 2010
yields another useful result which will be required later:

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\absolutevalue{\hgf(x)-f_0(x)}\to_{a.s.}0.

(83)

Next we show that
H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})\to_{a.s.}0
, which completes the proof of part A. To that end, note that

\displaystyle 2H^{2}(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(z-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{g_{0}(z-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}\bigg)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 4H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})^{2}+2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{g_{0}(z-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{g_{0}(z-\theta_{0}\vphantom{\widehat{\theta}_{n}})}\bigg)^{2}dz,

where the first term on the right hand side of the last display approaches zero almost surely by (
82
). The integrand in the second term is also bounded above by a constant multiple of
g_{0}(z-\widehat{\theta}_{n})+g_{0}(z-\theta_{0})
, which converges to
2f_{0}(z)
, and is integrable. Therefore, using Pratt’s lemma (Fact
10
), we deduce that the second term also converge to zero almost surely. Hence
H^{2}(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})\to_{a.s.}0
follows.

Now we turn to the proof of part B, where
we first establish that
H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-4/5})
. To that end, we first introduce the class of functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M,0}=\bigg\{f\in\mathcal{LC}\ \bigg|\

\displaystyle\ \sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)<M,\ \inf_{|x|>1}f(x)>1/M,\ \text{supp}(f)\subset\text{supp}(f_{0})\bigg\}.

We will show that without loss of generality, one can assume that
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{M,0}
for some
M>0.
To this end, we translate and rescale the data letting
\tilde{X}_{i}=\alpha X_{i}+\beta
, where
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Observe that the rescaled data has density
\tilde{f}_{0}(x)=\alpha^{-1}f_{0}((x-\beta)/\alpha)
. Denote by
\tilde{f}_{0,n}
the MLE of
f_{0}
based on the rescaled data. Note that the MLE is affine-equivalent, which entails that
\tilde{f}_{0,n}(x)=\alpha^{-1}\widehat{f}_{n}((x-\beta)/\alpha).
Noting Hellinger distance is invariant under affine transformations, we observe that
H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})=H(\tilde{f}_{0,n},\tilde{f}_{0}).

Therefore, it suffices to show that
H(\tilde{f}_{0,n},\tilde{f}_{0})\to_{a.s.}0
. Note that since
f_{0}
is log-concave,
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{dom}(f_{0}))
contains an interval. We can choose
\alpha
and
\beta
in a way such that
(x-\beta)/\alpha
lie inside that interval for
x=\pm 1
. Then it is possible to find
M>0
large enough such that

f_{0}((x-\beta)/\alpha)>\alpha/M,\ \text{ for }x=\pm 1,\quad{\text{yielding}}\quad\min(\tilde{f}_{0}(-1),\tilde{f}_{0}(1))>1/M.

The above implies

\inf_{x\in[-1,1]}\tilde{f}_{0}(x)>1/M
,
since
f_{0},
or equivalently
\tilde{f}_{0}
is unimodal. Hence, without loss of generality, we can assume that there exists
M>0
such that
f_{0}(x)>1/M
for
x\in[-1,1].

We can choose
M
large enough such that additionally,
\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f_{0}(x)<M
. On the other hand, (
83
) implies

\limsup_{n}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}_{n}(x)<M,\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n}\widehat{f}_{n}(\pm 1)>1/M.

Therefore,
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{M,0}
, and with probability one,
\widehat{f}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{M,0}
as well for all sufficiently large
n
.
Doss and Wellner 2016
obtained the bracketing entropy of the class
\mathcal{P}_{M,0}
. They showed that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{P}_{M,0},H)\lesssim\epsilon^{-1/2}.

The rest of the proof for
H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-2/5})
now follows from an application of Theorem
3.4.1
and
3.4.4
of
Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
.

Now we turn to establishing the rate of convergences of
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
and
\widehat{g}_{n}
. If
x-\theta_{0}
is a continuity point of
g_{0}^{\prime}
, using the fact that
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\to_{a.s.}\theta_{0}
, we obtain that

\dfrac{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}}{(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})}\to_{a.s.}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-\theta_{0})}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}}.

Noting
g_{0}^{\prime}
is continuous almost everywhere with respect to Lebesgue measure, and using Fatou’s lemma and part A of the current theorem, we obtain that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\dfrac{\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}\bigg)^{2}dx}{(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})^{2}}\geq\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-\theta_{0})}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}}\bigg)^{2}dx=\dfrac{\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}}{4}

(84)

with probability one. Now observe that

\displaystyle 2H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}\bigg)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}+\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}\bigg)^{2}dx+T_{c},

where

T_{c}=2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}\bigg)\bigg(\sqrt{g_{0}(x-\widehat{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x-\theta_{0})}\bigg)dx.

The inequality in (
84
) entails that for all sufficiently large
n
,

2H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})^{2}\geq 2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}+\dfrac{(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})^{2}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}}{4}-|T_{c}|\quad a.s.

We aim to show that the cross-term
|T_{c}|
is small. In fact, we show that

\dfrac{|T_{c}|}{|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}|^{2}+H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}}=o_{p}(1).

(85)

Suppose (
85
) holds. Then it follows that

\displaystyle 2H(\widehat{f}_{n},f_{0})^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\ 2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\ +\dfrac{(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})^{2}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}}{4}-o_{p}(1)H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}-o_{p}(1)(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})^{2},

which completes the proof because
\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}>0
.

Hence, it remains to prove (
85
). To this end, notice that
T_{c}
can be written as

\displaystyle T_{c}=2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x+\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})}\bigg)dx.

Recalling
\delta_{n}=\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}_{n}
, and noting
g_{0}
is absolutely continuous because
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, we can write

\displaystyle|T_{c}|=

\displaystyle\bigg|2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dt\bigg)dx\bigg|.

Since
g_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle|T_{c}|=

\displaystyle\ 2\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dt\bigg)dx

\displaystyle\ -\ \displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(-x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(-x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(-x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(-x-t)}}dt\bigg)dx\bigg|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dt\bigg)dx

\displaystyle\ -\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}dt\bigg)dx\bigg|,

yielding

|T_{c}|=2\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}-\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)dt\bigg)dx\bigg|.

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain that

\displaystyle\dfrac{|T_{c}|}{2|\delta_{n}|}\leq

\displaystyle\ \bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}\bigg)^{2}dx\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ \bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{1}{|\delta_{n}|}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}-\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)dt\bigg)^{2}dx\bigg)^{1/2}.

Since
\sqrt{\widehat{g}_{n}(x)}-\sqrt{\vphantom{\widehat{g}_{n}}g_{0}(x)}
is an even function, the first term on the right hand side of the last inequality is
\sqrt{2}H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0}).
Hence,

\displaystyle\dfrac{T_{c}^{2}}{8H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{n}}^{0}\dfrac{1}{|\delta_{n}|}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}-\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)dt\bigg)^{2}dx,

which, noting

t\mapsto\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}-\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}

is an even function for each
x>0
, can be bounded above by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|\delta_{n}|\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\dfrac{1}{(\delta_{n})^{2}}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}-\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{2\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)^{2}dt\bigg)dx

using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\dfrac{T_{c}^{2}}{2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle\ \dfrac{1}{|\delta_{n}|}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\bigg[\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx

\displaystyle\ -2\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx\bigg]dt.

(86)

For
t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx=\displaystyle\int_{t}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x)}}\bigg)^{2}dx\leq\dfrac{\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}}{2}.

Now observe that for
z\in(-|\delta_{n}|,0),

|g_{0}^{\prime}(z)/\sqrt{g_{0}(z)}|=|\psi_{0}^{\prime}(z)|\sqrt{g_{0}(z)}\leq|\psi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{n})|\sqrt{g_{0}(0)}=O_{p}(1),

(87)

since
\psi_{0}\in\mathcal{SC}_{0}
, and
\delta_{n}\to_{a.s.}0
.
Hence, for
t\in(0,|\delta_{n}|)
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx\ =

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-t}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(z)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(z)}}\bigg)^{2}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-t}^{0}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(z)}}\bigg)^{2}dz+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(z)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(z)}}\bigg)^{2}dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ |\delta_{n}|\psi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{n})^{2}\sqrt{g_{0}(0)}+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ |\delta_{n}|O_{p}(1)+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}/2,

where the last step follows from (
87
).
Hence, for any
t\in(0,|\delta_{n}|),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\bigg)^{2}dx=|\delta_{n}|O_{p}(1)+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}.

(88)

Our objective is to apply Fatou’s lemma on the third term on the right hand side of (
F
). Therefore, we want to ensure that the integrand is non-negative.
Note that when
x\geq|\delta_{n}|
and
t\in(0,|\delta_{n}|)
, we have
x>t
, which leads to

g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)\geq 0.

(89)

Keeping that in mind, we partition the term

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ -\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx+|\delta_{n}|O_{p}(1),

where the last step follows from (
87
).
The above combined with (
F
) and (
88
) leads to

\displaystyle\limsup_{n}\dfrac{T_{c}^{2}}{2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \limsup_{n}\dfrac{1}{|\delta_{n}|}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\bigg[|\delta_{n}|O_{p}(1)+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}-2\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dx\bigg]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ O_{p}(1)\limsup_{n}|\delta_{n}|+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}

\displaystyle\ -2\liminf_{n}\dfrac{1}{|\delta_{n}|}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 0+\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}-2\liminf_{n}\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}dt}{|\delta_{n}|}dx.

(90)

Therefore, an application of Fatou’s Lemma and (
89
) yield

\liminf_{n}\displaystyle\int_{|\delta_{n}|}^{\infty}\dfrac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|\delta_{n}|}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x+t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x+t)}}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x-t)}{\sqrt{g_{0}(x-t)}}dt}{|\delta_{n}|}dx\geq\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{g_{0}^{\prime}(x)^{2}}{g_{0}(x)}dx=\dfrac{\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}}{2}.

Thus (
F
) leads to

\dfrac{2T_{c}^{2}}{4H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}=o_{p}(1).

from which it is obvious that

\dfrac{\sqrt{2}|T_{c}|}{|\delta_{n}|^{2}+H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})^{2}}\leq\dfrac{\sqrt{2}|T_{c}|}{2H(\widehat{g}_{n},g_{0})|\delta_{n}|}=o_{p}(1),

which proves (
85
) and thus completes the proof of part B of Theorem
5
.
\Box

F.1
Auxilliary lemmas for Theorem
5

Proof of Lemma
F.1
.

The proof exactly follows the proof of Theorem
3.2
of
Pal et al. 2007
.
Since
\widehat{f}_{n}
is piecewise linear,
\widehat{f}_{n}
attains its maxima at some order statistic, say
X_{(m)}
. If
m>n/2,
set
m_{q}=[n/4]
where
[x]
is the greatest integer less than or equal to
x.
For
m\leq n/2,
we let
m_{q}=[3n/4]+1.
Set
K_{n}=m_{q}
or
n-m_{q},
accordingly as
m>n/2
or
\leq n/2.
It is easy to see that
n/K_{n}\to 4
as
n\to\infty
. Also,

\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})\leq\dfrac{1}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\bigg(1+\log\dfrac{\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})}{\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m_{q})})}\bigg)

(91)

by Lemma
3
of
Pal et al. 2007
(see our Lemma
F.2
). Now since

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{0}(X_{i}-\theta_{0})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\psi}_{n}(X_{i}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})\leq K_{n}\log(\hgf(X_{(m_q)}))+(n-K_{n})\log(\hgf(X_{(m)})),

K_{n}\log\dfrac{\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})}{\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m_{q})})}\leq n\bigg(\log\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})-l_{n}(\theta_{0},\psi_{0})/n\bigg).

(92)

Combining (
91
) and (
92
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})\leq

\displaystyle\ \dfrac{1}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\bigg(1+\dfrac{n}{K_{n}}\bigg(\log\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})-l_{n}(\theta_{0},\psi_{0})/n\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \dfrac{n/K_{n}}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\log\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})+\dfrac{1}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\bigg(1-\dfrac{l_{n}(\theta_{0},\psi_{0})}{K_{n}}\bigg).

Therefore by Lemma
4
of
Pal et al. 2007
(see our Lemma
F.3
),

\widehat{f}_{n}(X_{(m)})\leq\dfrac{2n/K_{n}}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\log\bigg(\dfrac{2n/K_{n}}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\bigg)+\dfrac{2}{|X_{(m)}-X_{(m_{q})}|}\bigg(1-\dfrac{l_{n}(\theta_{0},\psi_{0})}{K_{n}}\bigg)

which is finite by our choices of
m
,
m_{q}
and
K_{n}.

∎

The following lemmas appear in
Pal et al. 2007
as Lemma
3
and
4
respectively.

Lemma F.2
.

Suppose
f
is a log-concave density. If
0<f(x)\leq f(y)
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R},
then

f(y)\leq\dfrac{1+\log(f(y)/f(x))}{|y-x|}.

Lemma F.3
.

If
x,c_{1},c_{2}>0
and

x\leq c_{1}\log x+c_{2},

then

x\leq 2c_{1}\log(2c_1)+2c_{2}
.

G
Technical facts

Below we list some facts which have been used repeatedly in our proofs. We begin with a well-known fact on total variation distance.

Fact 5
.

Suppose
F
and
G
are two distribution functions with densities
f
and
g
, respectively. Then

d_{TV}(F,G)\leq\sqrt{2}H(f,g).

Fact 6

(Theorem 5.7 (ii) of
Shorack 2000
)
.

Suppose
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a random sequence. If
X_{n}
satisfies
X_{n}\to_{p}X
for some random variable
X
, then there exists a subsequence
n_{k}
such that
X_{n_{k}}\to_{a.s.}X
.

Fact 7

(Theorem 5.7 (vii) of
Shorack 2000
)
.

Suppose
X_{n}
is a sequence of random variables.
Then for some random variable
X
,
X_{n}\to_{p}X
if and only if every subsequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
contains
a further subsequence
\{n_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}
for which
X_{n_{r}}\to_{a.s.}X
.

Fact 8

(Proposition A.18 of
Bobkov and Ledoux 2014
)
.

Suppose the density
f
is supported on an open interval (possibly unbounded). Then
F^{-1}
is strictly increasing, and
F^{-1}(q_{2})-F^{-1}(q_{1})=\int_{q_{1}}^{q_{2}}dt/f(F^{-1}(t))
for all
0<q_{1}<q_{2}<1
.

Fact 9
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a class of measurable functions
h
such that
\int h^{2}dP_{0}<\epsilon^{2}
where
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq M
for some constant
M>0
. Then

E\|\mathbb{G}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{F}}\lesssim J_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P_{0}))\bigg(1+\frac{MJ_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P_{0}))}{\epsilon^{2}\sqrt{n}}\bigg)

where

J_{[\ ]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P_{0}))=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\sqrt{1+\log N_{[\ ]}(\epsilon^{\prime},\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P_{0}))}d\epsilon^{\prime}.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma 3.4.2, pp. 324 of
Van der Vaart 1998
.
∎

The next fact is Pratt’s lemma
(
Pratt 1960
, Theorem 1)
.
We state it here for convenience.

Fact 10
.

Suppose
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
is a measure space and
a_{n},b_{n},c_{n}
are sequences of functions on
\Omega
converging almost everywhere to functions
a,b,c
respectively. Also, all functions are integrable and

\int a_{n}d\mu\to\int ad\mu
and
\int c_{n}d\mu\to\int cd\mu.
Moreover,
a_{n}\leq b_{n}\leq c_{n}.
Then

\int b_{n}d\mu\to\int bd\mu
.

Fact 11
.

Suppose
(F_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
F
are distribution functions satisfying
\|F_{n}-F\|_{\infty}\to 0
. Further suppose
F
has density
f
and
t\in\mathrm{int}(\text{supp}(f))
. Then

|F_{n}^{-1}(t)-F^{-1}(t)|\to 0
.

Proof.

Since
F^{-1}
is continuous at
t
, this is essentially Lemma A.5 of
Bobkov and Ledoux 2014
.
∎

The following is a property of integrable functions.

Fact 12

(Exercise 16.18, pp. 223 of
Billingsley 1986
)
.

Suppose
P
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h|dP<\infty
for some measurable function
h
. Then for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\sigma>0
so that any
P
-measurable set
\mathcal{B}
with
P(\mathcal{B})<\sigma
satisfies

\int_{\mathcal{B}}|h|dP<\epsilon
.

The following is a sufficient (and necessary) condition for uniform integrability.

Fact 13

(Exercise 16.19, pp. 223 of
Billingsley 1986
)
.

Suppose
P
is a finite measure on
\mathbb{R}
and
(h_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of
P
-measurable functions. Then
(h_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable if and only if (i)
\sup_{n\geq 1}\int|h_{n}|dP<\infty
(ii) given any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\sigma>0
so that any
P
-measurable set
\mathcal{B}
with
P(\mathcal{B})<\sigma
satisfies

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{\mathcal{B}}|h_{n}|dP<\epsilon
.

The following fact is a Glivenko-Cantelli type result for a class of functions
\mathcal{F}_{n}
changing with
n
.

Fact 14
.

Suppose

\mathcal{F}_{n}

is a class of functions such that
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\|f\|_{\infty}\leq M_{n}
. Further suppose for any fixed
\epsilon>0
,

M_{n}^{2}\sup_{Q}\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{n},L_{2}(Q))=o(n)
where the supremum is over all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
. Then

E\|\mathbb{P}_{n}-P\|_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof of Fact
14
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem 2.4.3 of
Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
. Therefore we only highlight the differences. Suppose
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}P
. Consider also
n
independent Rademacher random variables
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
. Using the symmetrization inequality
(cf. Lemma 2.3.1 of
Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
, )
and Fubini’s theorem, one can show that

E\|\mathbb{P}_{n}-P\|_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\leq 2E_{X}\underbrace{E_{\epsilon}\norm{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\e_i f(X_i)}_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}}_{\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)\equiv\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})},

where
E_{X}
and
E_{\epsilon}
denote the expectations with respect to
P
and the law of
\epsilon_{1}
, respectively. Fixing
\delta>0
, and using the argument in the proof of Theorem 2.4.3 of
Van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
, we can show that

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)\leq(1+\log N(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{n},L_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{n}))^{1/2}M_{n}\sqrt{6/n}+\delta

(93)

where
\mathbb{F}_{n}
is the empirical distribution function of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.
Taking
\delta=1/2
, for sufficiently large
n
, we have

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)\leq 1

for any realizations of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. Therefore
\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)
is a bounded sequence. For any
\delta>0
, (
93
) also implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)\leq\delta
. Since
\delta
is arbitrary, this implies
\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
for any realization of
X\equiv X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. Therefore, using dominated convergence theorem we conclude that
E_{X}[\mathbb{Y}_{n}(X)]\to_{n}0
.
∎

Fact 15
.

Suppose
f_{0}
is a log-concave density with
\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}<\infty
. Then
H(f_{0}(\mathord{\cdot}+y),f_{0})=O(|y|)
.

Proof of Fact
15
.

Note that

H(f_{0}(\mathord{\cdot}+y),f_{0})^{2}=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sqrt{f_{0}(x+y)}-\sqrt{f_{0}(x)})^{2}dx\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{x-|y|}^{x+|y|}\frac{|f_{0}^{\prime}(z)|}{2\sqrt{f_{0}(z)}}dz\bigg)^{2}dx,

which, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, is bounded above by

\frac{|y|}{4}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x-|y|}^{x+|y|}\frac{f_{0}^{\prime}(z)^{2}}{f_{0}(z)}dzdx\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi^{\prime}_{0}(z)^{2}f_{0}(z)dz=|y|^{2}\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}/2,

where (a) follows by Fubini’s Theorem.
Since
\mathcal{I}_{f_{0}}<\infty
, the above is of order
O(|y|^{2})
.
∎

Fact 16
.

Suppose
0\leq u,v\leq 1/2
. Then it holds that

u(1-u)+v(1-v)\geq\min\Big(\frac{u+v}{2},1-\frac{u+v}{2}\Big).

Proof of Fact
16
.

Suppose
0\leq u,v\leq 1/2
. Since
u(1-u)\geq\min\{u,1-u\}/2
, it follows that

\displaystyle u(1-u)+v(1-v)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\min\{u,1-u\}+\frac{1}{2}\min\{v,1-v\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\min\bigg(\frac{u}{2},\frac{1}{4}\bigg)+\min\bigg(\frac{v}{2},\frac{1}{4}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{u}{2}+\frac{v}{2}=\frac{u+v}{2}

where (a) follows because
1-u,1-v\geq 1/2
for
0\leq u,v\leq 1/2
.
Similarly, for
1/2\leq u,v\leq 1
,

\displaystyle u(1-u)+v(1-v)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\min\{u,1-u\}+\frac{1}{2}\min\{v,1-v\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\min\bigg(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1-u}{2}\bigg)+\min\bigg(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1-v}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-u}{2}+\frac{1-v}{2}=1-\frac{u+v}{2}

where (a) follows because
u,v\geq 1/2
. Hence, the proof follows.
∎

H
Tuning parameters for
Stone 1975
and
Beran 1974
’s estimators

Stone 1975
’s estimator has two tuning parameters
d_{n}
and
t_{n}
. To find the optimal
(d_{n},t_{n})
pair, we implement a grid search on a two dimensional grid. Each point on the grid is of the form
(d,t)
where
d\in\{10,20,30,\ldots,80\}
, and
t_{n}\in\{0.10,0.20,\ldots,0.60\}
. For each distribution and each sample size, we estimate the efficiency of each pair using one hundred Monte Carlo samples. The optimal pair is the one which maximizes the estimated efficiency.
Since
Beran 1974
’s estimator also uses two tuning parameters
b_{c,n}
and
\rho_{n}
, we repeat the same procedure for finding the optimal tuning parameters. The only difference is that in this case, the scaling parameter is chosen from the grid
\{0.10,0.20,\ldots,1.50\}
, and the number of basis functions is allowed to vary within the set
\{10,20,\ldots,50\}
.
Table
2
and
3
tabulate the optimal tuning parameters that we obtained following the above-mentioned procedure.

I
Corrections from the previous arxived version

1.

Previously, the proof of Fact
4
incorrectly stated that the function mapping
(x,y)\mapsto xy
is convex on the set
\{(x,y):x\geq 0,\ y\geq 0\}
. However, this function is not convex. We have removed the part of the proof that relied on this assertion. Now we use the following algebraic inequality:

u(1-u)+v(1-v)\geq\min\left(\frac{u+v}{2},1-\frac{u+v}{2}\right)\quad\text{for}\quad u,v\in[0,1/2]

to prove Fact 4 instead. The above algebraic fact
is now given in Fact
16
.

2.

The term
(\log n)^{2}
in the statement of Lemma B.12 was incorrect; it is now corrected to
(\log n)^{4}
. This correction resulted in changes to the power of the
\log(n)
terms in the statements of several auxiliary lemmas for proving Theorems
1
and
2
, namely Lemma B.12, Lemma B.14, Lemma B.16, Lemma D.4, and Lemma D.7. Some minor corrections were also made in the proof of the first step of Theorems
1
and
2
due to these changes. In the proof of Theorem
1
, the
h_{n}
defined in (
23
) now has an
L_{2}(P_{0})
norm
\|h_{n}\|_{P_{0},2}
of the order
O_{p}(n^{-2p/5}(\log n)^{3})
, which previously was of the order
O_{p}(n^{-2p/5}(\log n)^{3/2})
. Similarly, the corrected order of the
L_{2}(P_{0})
norm of
h_{n}
defined in (
72
) in the proof of Theorem
2
is
O_{p}(n^{-2p/5}(\log n)^{3})
.
However, these minor alterations did not affect the statements of Theorems
1
and
2
.

3.

In the previous version, we let
p\in(0,1)
in its definition in Condition
2
. However, since
O_{p}(n^{-p})
is the rate of Hellinger decay for nonparametric estimation of
g_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
,
p
is expected to be less than
1/2
. When
\theta_{0}
is known, the conjectured minimax rate of Hellinger error decay for nonparametrically estimating a symmetric log-concave density is
O_{p}(n^{-2/5})

(
Doss and Wellner 2019b
)
. Therefore, in the current version, we set
p\in(0,1/2]
when we define it in Condition
2
.

4.

There was an algebraic mistake in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
1
while bounding
E\left[\|\mathbb{G}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(C)}\right]
(page 30). This has now been corrected. To elaborate, previously, we incorrectly deduced
K_{n}^{-1}M_{n}^{2}n^{-1/2}
to be of the order
O((\log n)^{-3/2}n^{-1/2})
. However, after all corrections, the rate turns out to be

K_{n}^{-1}M_{n}^{2}n^{-1/2}=O((\log n)^{-3}n^{(8p-5)/10}),

which goes to zero because
p\leq 1/2
as per its current definition in Condition
2
.

5.

Several typographical mistakes were corrected, including the definition of the Hellinger distance on page 4.

6.

Some references to books or other papers were made more explicit by adding page numbers to them.
